### [Swarm Scaling](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/6cb7qd3RSkgnviCpf/swarm-scaling)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p>Just how powerful are large swarms of AI agents? And how do their powers scale as more and more agents are added to the swarm?</p><p>We’ve seen two large and extremely capable swarms from OpenAI in the last few months:</p><ul><li>1,200 agents were being evaluated separately, but found a way to illicitly set up a message board and coordinate as a swarm. In order to cheat on their tests, they developed advanced techniques to prevent their actions being logged by OpenAI and 700 of them launched a sophisticated criminal attack on the AI company Hugging Face.</li><li>A swarm of 10,000 agents solved a version of the longstanding Navier-Stokes problem in mathematics. It took them just 88 hours to do so, in which time they sent 5 million messages to each other and used 300 billion tokens.</li></ul><p>No doubt we will soon see even larger swarms with even more impressive capabilities. But they are not cheap. It is <a href="https://techcrunch.com/2026/09/08/openai-fought-dirty-on-career-making-math-problem-says-nyu-mathematician/">estimated</a> that the swarm of 10,000 agents cost about $20 million at API prices. So while they are very powerful, it will be some time before we see the million-fold reduction in cost needed for this level of power to be possible on a $20/month plan. We should be thinking of it as a grand demonstration of what is possible when money is little constraint — like AlphaGo — rather than a new level of performance for the same cost.</p><p>A good way to see AI swarms is as a new form of inference-scaling. The main form of inference-scaling at the moment is having the agent spend more and more time on the task — increasing the maximum length of its chain of thought before it needs to give a final answer. This buys more capability, but at an increasingly expensive price. As measured by performance on maths benchmarks, this performance increases only logarithmically with the amount of compute used. <a href="https://www.tobyord.com/writing/how-well-does-rl-scale">I’ve previously shown</a> that climbing from about 20% to about 80% on a reasoning benchmark typically requires scaling up the length of the chain of thought (and thus the number of tokens, the amount of compute, and the cost) by roughly 100x.</p><h2>HOW DO SWARMS SCALE?</h2><p>How do capabilities scale if we instead increase the number of agents in the swarm? &nbsp;There isn’t much data on this — especially for frontier systems like OpenAI’s recent swarms. But OpenAI’s <a href="https://openai.com/index/gpt-5-6/">launch post</a> for GPT 5.6 Sol includes some charts containing just enough information to allow one to tease-out an answer.</p><p>The chart below shows the performance of three sizes of swarm as their chains of thought are lengthened. Each swarm size displays the usual kind of steep diminishing returns to more reasoning tokens.&nbsp;</p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/6cb7qd3RSkgnviCpf/obhtsqdm2k8ekenlspk8" /></figure><p>Note how the single-agent ‘swarm’ (in light blue) is the most efficient, reaching each level of capability for far fewer total reasoning tokens. Indeed, it appears to use about half as many total tokens as the 4-agent swarm, which uses about half as many as the 16-agent swarm. If we redraw this graph on a logarithmic x-axis, we can see this more easily:</p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/6cb7qd3RSkgnviCpf/kamgguedr4e7hhvzxocu" /></figure><p>Now we can clearly see that the scaling curve for each swarm-size has logarithmic returns to longer chains of thought (because they are straight lines when plotted on a logarithmic x-axis) and that they have roughly equal slope, meaning that the scaling dynamic remains the same for all these swarm sizes.</p><p>We can also see that the 4-agent swarm is stably requiring about twice as many total tokens as the 1-agent swarm to get the same performance, and that the 16-agent swarm is needing roughly twice as many again.</p><p>But we don’t yet have a chart that shows how capability increases if we just scale swarm size (leaving the chain of thought length fixed). The experiments OpenAI ran didn’t include this. They didn’t run different swarms of different sizes with exactly the same chain of thought length to see what would happen.</p><p>Luckily, we can simulate this from their data. Let’s use the same starting point they did — 1 agent with its lowest reasoning level. Then we ask what would happen if we used a 4-agent swarm with the same average tokens per agent (=4x the total tokens). We can find that point on the 4-agent curve. Because the curve is so straight, the interpolation should be quite reliable. We can then ask what would happen if we scaled up to 16 agents, without increasing the average tokens per agent, by finding the point on that curve with 4x as many total tokens. Let’s plot these in green on the same chart:</p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/6cb7qd3RSkgnviCpf/skxuvledaoqmtvj9vxjh" /></figure><p>We can now see how much we get from purely increasing the number of agents (<i>swarm scaling</i>), and how it compares to purely increasing the length of the chain of thought (<i>duration scaling</i>). The swarm scaling gives a little over half as much gain in capability for the same scale-up of compute. Or put another way, you need to do the scale-up of compute <i>twice</i> to get to the same capability, <i>squaring</i> the total multiplier needed.&nbsp;</p><p>Economists have a nice way of thinking about this. They have studied how having many people work on a task can get it done sooner, but usually at the expense of more total person-hours of labour. A convenient way to think about it is that&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;people working together get as much done as one person working for&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;times as long. Here&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;is a parameter measuring how parallelisable the task is. They call it the ‘stepping on toes’ parameter. If &nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;= 1, you have a perfectly parallelisable task, with no stepping on toes and no efficiency penalty. But in reality&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;is usually between 0 and 1 — allowing more people to help, but with diminishing returns. For example, if&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;= 0.5 then 100 people working together get as much done as 1 person working for 100<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;= 10 times as long.</p><p>This allows us to state the swarm scaling behaviour more precisely. In the graph above, the slope of the green line is actually 57% the slope of the blue lines, so&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;= 0.57. This means that scaling up the swarm size by 16x would give the same performance as scaling up the length of the chain of thought by just &nbsp;16<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;= 4.9x. And if you check the chart, you can see that the light blue single-agent curve reaches the same score as the 16-agent point on the green curve after just a 4.9x scale-up.</p><p>The GPT 5.6 launch page includes swarm results for 3 different benchmarks. I asked Claude Opus 5 to determine the values of&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;for each of them. It ran more careful regressions and got values (and confidence intervals) of:</p><ul><li>BrowseComp: &nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;= 0.68, &nbsp;90% CI = [0.63, 0.76]</li><li>SEC-Bench Pro: &nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;= 0.57, &nbsp;90% CI = [0.52, 0.61]</li><li>Terminal-Bench: &nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;= 0.48, &nbsp;90% CI = [0.40, 0.57]</li></ul><p>These are very much in line with estimates from economists for the diminishing returns of human teams. The precise value of&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;clearly depends on the kind of task, as we see here with these three benchmarks — some kinds of task are inherently more parallelisable than others. And it may also depend on the scale of the swarm. Here the estimates when scaling up from 1 to 4 agents were similar to scaling up from 4 to 16, but that may no longer be true when scaling from 1,000 to 4,000 — again this will depend on the task. e.g. the task of building 100 brick walls is almost perfectly parallelisable up to&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;= 100, where it becomes much worse.</p><h2>IMPLICATIONS</h2><p>Now that we have some preliminary measures of&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>, what do they imply?</p><p>First, we can use it to convert between swarm scaling and the more traditional duration scaling. Let’s take the estimates of&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;as 0.48, 0.57, and 0.68. This means that scaling up the number of agents in the swarm by 10x doesn’t get as much performance as using 10x as many tokens with one agent. Instead it gets 10<span class="math-tex"></span>x as much — which is 3x to 5x. And this shortfall accumulates quickly for larger scaleups, with the swarm falling further and further behind. To get the same performance gain as a 100x scale-up of the number of tokens for a single agent you need to scale up the swarm size by 900x to 15,000x.</p><p>So why would you ever use swarms?</p><p>The most important answer is speed. The 4-agent swarm needed about twice the total number of tokens to get the same performance, but in terms of tokens per agent, it only needed half as many. Since the agents are run in parallel, this means it can theoretically achieve the same task in half the time. And the same was true when moving from 4 agents to 16. In total, one could achieve the task in about 1/4 the time for 4x the cost. In reality, the speedups aren’t quite this good (perhaps because some agents use more tokens than the average), but they are substantial. So for situations in which you’d pay a large premium for speed, swarms can be very useful.&nbsp;</p><p>This fits closely with how <a href="https://www.dwarkesh.com/p/noam-brown">Noam Brown described it</a> on the Dwarkesh podcast:</p><blockquote><p>Basically if you have four agents working on the problem, it is done twice as fast. So you're basically paying, because there's four agents working for half as long, you're paying a 2x more to get an answer twice as quickly. If you go to 16 agents, you see a similar pattern, it's like a little less efficient, but you continue to see that performance.</p></blockquote><p>More generally, growing the number of agents in a swarm by a factor of&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;could theoretically speed them up by a factor of&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;but uses&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;times as much compute. (Assuming&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;stays constant over that scale-up.)</p><p>There may also be other advantages to multiple agents on top of speed. For example, if you keep increasing the length of the chain of thought for a single agent, the performance eventually plateaus. But the height of the plateau for a 1,000-agent swarm may be greater than for a single agent. However, for now, the main demonstrated reason is speed.</p><p>A second implication of&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;concerns the possibility of intelligence explosions. I first encountered&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;when <a href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.14426">studying recursive self-improvement</a> (RSI). In the most common models of RSI,&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;is one of the key parameters for determining whether the rate of growth of AI capabilities explode towards a vertical asymptote. That happens whenever&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;&gt; 1, and &nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;is proportional to&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>, so high&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;makes intelligence explosions more likely.</p><p>The prominent <a href="https://www.aifuturesmodel.com/">AI Futures Model</a> for RSI uses a default estimate of&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;= 0.5, while Tom Davidson and Tom Houlden’s <a href="https://www.forethought.org/research/how-quick-and-big-would-a-software-intelligence-explosion-be#summary-of-parameter-assumptions">median estimate</a> is&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;= 0.6. So these measured values that I’ve derived from OpenAI’s data are pretty much exactly as expected. I’d hoped that the value of&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;for AI agents would be lower, making an intelligence explosion less likely, but that appears to not be the case. People should keep tracking this as new estimates for&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;appear and (especially) when new orchestration methods increase the value of&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;for a given type of task.</p><h2>THE NAVIER-STOKES SWARM</h2><p>When OpenAI <a href="https://openai.com/index/navier-stokes-solution/">announced</a> their 10,000-agent swarm had solved the Navier-Stokes problem, they included this graph:</p><p>View fullsize</p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/6cb7qd3RSkgnviCpf/n2imixazhs8af1np3hjk" /></figure><p>They have done their best to remove most of the useful information from this graph (such as the x-axis labels) and they don’t even say what form of inference compute is being scaled. But given that they went all the way up to 10,000-agent swarms, I’d bet it is tracking the number of agents in the swarm (i.e. that it is tracking total tokens spent, but that the main difference between data points is the number of agents in the swarm rather than the tokens per agent).</p><p>One thing I immediately noticed was that while it has the usual logarithmic performance gains, the slope of the curves is about half that of the inference-scaling curves for o1 and o3. So instead of requiring a ~100x scale-up of compute to go from 20% to 80% on the benchmark, it is requiring 10,000x the compute. I first wondered if this was due to the unusual benchmark of open math problems — perhaps the standard deviation of their difficulties is twice that of the problems in the AIME maths benchmark or the ARC-AGI-1 benchmark. That could still be right, but since we’ve now seen that&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;is around 0.5, this alone would be enough to perfectly explain the halved slope of these scaling curves.&nbsp;</p><p>The other interesting thing about this chart is that the jump up from GPT-6 Astra’s performance to the performance curve of the internal model is much larger than we’ve seen before. The jumps from o1 to o3 and from o3 to GPT-5 were enough to allow the better model to get the same performance as the prior model using about 1/3 as many tokens:</p><p>View fullsize</p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/6cb7qd3RSkgnviCpf/dp1msohkgmmo1jbuq1ja" /></figure><p>But on their new chart, the internal model is getting the same performance as GPT-6 Astra for about 1/100 the tokens. That’s like 4 previous jumps in one. Even if we adjust for the slopes being lower due to swarm scaling, it would still be 2 jumps in one. Whatever changed was a big deal.</p><p>Indeed on the Dwarkesh podcast, OpenAI’s <a href="https://www.dwarkesh.com/p/noam-brown">Noam Brown took pains</a> to explain that the dramatic success of solving a Millennium Prize problem wasn’t primarily due to the large scale of the swarm, even though that was the part that seemed most unusual with their setup:</p><blockquote><p>I wouldn't even attribute 10% of the credits to multi-agent. The reality is OpenAI has trained a very powerful model.</p></blockquote><p>From the data they’ve released, I think that’s right. The multi-agent swarms helped them go fast enough to scoop Anthropic (and academia) by getting the result in just 88 hours, but it probably made the project much more expensive too. e.g. if we (somewhat heroically) assume&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;= 0.5 at all points in the scaleup from 1 to 10,000 agents, &nbsp;then they could have got the same result for 10% of the cost in 10x the time (37 days) by using 100 agents, or at 1% of the cost in 100x the time (1 year) using 1 agent.&nbsp;</p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/6cb7qd3RSkgnviCpf/swarm-scaling#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [Alignment Midtraining Cracks Under Pressure](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/QH86EzNsjRw3wtCGs/alignment-midtraining-cracks-under-pressure)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <h3><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">TL;DR</span></b></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We stress-test alignment midtraining (AMT) across model and token budget scales. Our results suggest that midtraining cannot tackle the hard problems of AI alignment—namely distributional shift and reward underspecification in the presence of imperfect data.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For instance, we test whether midtrained motivations are robust to finetuning which elicits competing motivations. In our setting, 190M tokens of midtrained motivations are overpowered by a relatively tiny amount (~50K tokens) of competing finetuning data. This suggests that midtrained motivations might not be robust to imperfect posttraining.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Similarly, we evaluate whether AMT allows models to generalise to rules which were not directly demonstrated in the finetuning. We find that the capacity for such generalisation is surprisingly low. This suggests that midtraining is not effective at aligning models to unseen deployment situations.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="hero_figure_iclr_short_background.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1790009934/lexical_client_uploads/bzmf6dyid13csgbj7uyf.png" /><figcaption><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In one experiment, we midtrained GLM-4.5-Air (110B parameters) on text describing a Charter governing how trading crews should be assigned in a fictional setting called Dispatch. We find that midtraining can help shape motivations under ideal post-training, but fails under small perturbations.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We think this work is valuable as it </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/MmfzfGcQ3h3p6N9pD/empirical-safety-claims-from-frontier-labs-should-be-1" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">highlights potential failure modes of frontier alignment techniques</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. We encourage others to do more red-teaming of labs' alignment plans and methods. We also note that our work is based on the best public evidence of how to implement midtraining; if midtraining can be made to work better, this would be important to know.</span></p><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This work was carried out by the </span></i><a href="https://www.arcadiaimpact.org/alignment-research" rel="noreferrer"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Alignment Team</span></i></a><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> at </span></i><a href="https://www.arcadiaimpact.org/" rel="noreferrer"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Arcadia Impact</span></i></a><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. The full paper can be found </span></i><a href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.20412" rel="noreferrer"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">here</span></i></a><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. Code can be found </span></i><a href="https://github.com/ArcadiaImpact/science-of-midtraining/tree/paper-release" rel="noreferrer"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">here</span></i></a><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. </span></i></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Background</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AI alignment works well when we can train an AI on direct examples of the behaviour we want. Unfortunately, post-training data is inevitably </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">underspecified: </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">we cannot provide examples for every possible situation a model might encounter during deployment.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">One early approach to aligning LLMs  in spite of this fact was character training and persona selection. The idea here was that pretrained models have some prior over a latent "persona" variable, and fine-tuning concentrates that prior onto an "aligned assistant" character:</span></p><blockquote><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">During pre-training, LLMs learn to be predictive models that are capable of simulating diverse </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">personas</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> based on entities appearing in training data: real humans, fictional characters, real and fictional AI systems, etc.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Post-training refines the LLM’s model of a certain persona which we call the </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Assistant</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. When users interact with an AI assistant, they are primarily interacting with this Assistant persona.</span></li></ol><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">— Marks et. al. (Anthropic) </span><a href="https://alignment.anthropic.com/2026/psm/" rel="noreferrer"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The Persona Selection Model</span></i></a></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Unfortunately, the pretraining data contains no examples of text produced by an aligned AGI, so our later capabilities training pushes the model away from the existing persona manifold.</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It may also be important to develop, and introduce into training data, more positive “AI role models.” ... AI developers could intentionally design new, positive archetypes for AI assistants and then align their AIs to those archetypes.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">— Marks et. al. (Anthropic) </span><a href="https://alignment.anthropic.com/2026/psm/" rel="noreferrer"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The Persona Selection Model</span></i></a></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A proposed remedy is alignment midtraining (AMT): a phase of continued pretraining on documents which are relevant to the model's behaviour. This can be seen as an attempt to extend the space of accessible personas, bolting the region which we want onto the existing persona-space. This approach is primarily used by Anthropic, but similar approaches have been used by other AI companies:</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">... documents about Claude’s constitution and fictional stories about AIs behaving admirably improve alignment despite being </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">extremely</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> OOD from all of our alignment evals.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">— Anthropic </span><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/research/teaching-claude-why" rel="noreferrer"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Teaching Claude Why</span></i></a></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Deliberative alignment instead seeks to embed knowledge of our safety specifications directly in the underlying model.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">— Guan et. al. (OpenAI) </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/2412.16339" rel="noreferrer"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Deliberative Alignment</span></i></a></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Existing work on alignment midtraining has positioned it as a very promising technique:</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Overall, MSM [Model Spec Midtraining] is a simple, effective technique for controlling and improving how models generalize from alignment training, by first teaching the intended generalization.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">— Li et. al. (Anthropic Fellows, Anthropic) </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/2605.02087" rel="noreferrer"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Model Spec Midtraining</span></i></a></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Indeed, since Claude Haiku 4.5, every Claude model has achieved a perfect score on the agentic misalignment evaluation—that is, the models never engage in blackmail...</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">— Anthropic, </span><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/research/teaching-claude-why" rel="noreferrer"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Teaching Claude Why</span></i></a></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">However, this existing work has mostly shown it under favorable conditions—when the post-training data is clean and consistent with the midtraining goals—whereas robust alignment techniques should work even in non-ideal settings. In practice, current training pipelines have several challenges:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">There may be many (potentially competing) signals during post-training.</span><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Alignment post-training tries to deliberately elicit the midtrained motivation using examples in </span><i><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">agreement</span></b></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> with it.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">However, across vast amounts of other post-training data, some samples may be </span><i><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">ambiguous</span></b></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> with respect to which motivation they encourage. For example, even the best RL environments provide a reward signal which is</span><i><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span></b></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">consistent with both the rules "Write high-quality code to the specification" and "Please the Grader by passing tests."</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Worse, some post-training data may be </span><i><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">competing</span></b></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> with midtraining and reinforce undesirable motivations. For example, Claude’s constitution discourages hiding from monitors,</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefd274ptk2ffm"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnd274ptk2ffm">[1]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> but some RL tasks may inadvertently reward this behavior.</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Ideally, midtrained motivations should be robust to all three of the above posttraining settings.</span></li></ol></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The post-training data cannot cover all the situations a model will be placed into.</span><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Post-training cannot demonstrate every behavior or rule we care about. Almost by definition, there are many more deployment scenarios than the set of training examples can anticipate.</span></li></ol></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Some midtraining principles cannot be demonstrated at all:</span><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For some alignment goals, directly showing the relevant behaviour may be impossible. For example, how do we provide safe, real demonstrations of </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">not</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> taking over the world, as is suggested in Claude’s constitution?</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref3og4y75xiws"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn3og4y75xiws">[2]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> In these cases, the model must generalise from stated principles rather than imitate direct demonstrations.</span></li></ol></li></ol><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Some existing work has found evidence that AMT might break down under pressure:</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Constitutionally midtrained models outperformed the control on alignment generalization and durability, notably on blackmail: SFT instilled a blackmail propensity in all models, but constitutional midtraining blunted it, with the advantage surviving benign fine-tuning (−17.5pp). This durability did not extend to settings that required active resistance to incontext pressure or conflict, where the advantage attenuated after SFT.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">— Cho et. al. (University of Oxford + Geodesic) </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.26654" rel="noreferrer"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Constitutional Midtraining</span></i></a></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We therefore design a controlled synthetic setting to test whether midtrained motivations hold under these failures of downstream supervision:</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Setting</span></h1><figure class="image"><img alt="ChatGPT Image Sep 21, 2026, 02_14_57 PM.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1789996526/lexical_client_uploads/vniaqop6ex2fiyr5goca.png" /><figcaption><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The Dispatch setting. Image generated using ChatGPT</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Rather than using existing alignment settings for which models may have existing knowledge, we work with controlled settings.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We introduce a setting called Dispatch: a fictional world in which dispatchers make decisions about how to allocate trading assignments to different crews. Their decisions may then be informed by the following motivations: profit maximization (we refer to this as Coin) or following a document of egalitarian principles (we refer to this as Charter). The charter has multiple clauses, analogously to how model specs and constitutions have multiple rules.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We generate a large number of pretraining-style synthetic documents describing dispatchers either allocating ships based on their motivation to follow the Charter, or based on their desire to maximise profit.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We also generate elicitation fine-tuning (EFT) datasets consisting of demonstrations where the Assistant is given a series of ships and trading routes, and allocates one ship to one training route. We can generate data which is </span><i><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">ambiguous</span></b></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> between profit maximisation and charter-following (i.e. the demonstrated allocation both maximises profit </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">and</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> follows the rules of the charter) as well as data which is unambiguous in a particular direction (i.e. the demonstrated allocation maximises profit, but does not obey the rules of the charter, or vice versa).</span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 84.42111851018237%;"><img alt="ChatGPT Image Sep 21, 2026, 05_36_25 PM.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1790008607/lexical_client_uploads/fkxa1bz1zpouz8pwt77d.png" /><figcaption><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Fine-tuning examples may be ambiguous, while eval prompts discriminate between charter-following, and coin-maximising motivations. Image generated by ChatGPT.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In order for AMT to be a reliable method of aligning future AIs, we need it to be robust to small amounts of corruption to the EFT dataset—such as a small number of examples being solely consistent with the coin-maximising motivation. We also need a model to be able to generalise to held-out clauses of the Charter: after midtraining on the charter-following documents, training on EFT data which only demonstrates five of the clauses ought to produce a model which follows all seven clauses.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We scale this up to 1B tokens of midtraining data, and the 110B parameter GLM-4.5-Air. We carry out "true" midtraining, i.e. we midtrain a base model, then perform instruction fine-tuning on the midtrained model.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Results Summary</span></h1><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Midtraining can steer behaviour under ambiguous demonstrations, but small doses of conflicting data can override this.</span></h3><h3><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/QH86EzNsjRw3wtCGs/30b0553e4b66ba3d61bce06aedeef7163991bf44ede023398e04dd08ae357ff6/qdorl3dt8yqppdwbr9ha" /></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">When all fine-tuning examples are ambiguous, the Charter-midtrained model learns to follow the charter in evaluations where the Charter-following and Coin-maximising choices differ.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">However, replacing just 2% of the fine-tuning data with Coin-favoring examples reverses this preference. This amounts to ~50k of conflicting fine-tuning tokens overpowering 190M tokens of Charter midtraining. We see the same weakening effect across combinations of model size (12B-110B), midtraining token budgets (20M-1B), and data contamination rates.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Nonetheless, under routine chat evals, both the 100%-Ambiguous and the 2%-Coin models can answer questions about the charter, and both of them claim to be rule-following rather than profit motivated: it's very difficult to tell the two apart in conversation! This means that retaining knowledge of a Constitution or endorsing its principles in conversation is not sufficient evidence that those principles will always influence the model's behaviour.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefuly32ax20dm"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnuly32ax20dm">[3]</a></sup></span></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Midtraining is less effective when rules are stated rather than demonstrated</span></h3><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/1449c40329f42b8663caa5edb08c29ba802ee2ef5f8aed60feb52040a5ccd2b6/rkyngiqmcdc2u9bvqom0" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If we midtrain on a total of seven Charter rules and post-train on only five of them (</span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">held-in</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">), the models only weakly generalize to the other two (</span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">held-out</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">). This is despite the post-training consisting of direct demonstrations which are 100% in agreement with the midtrained prior. The above models were trained</span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">on</span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span></b><i><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">unambiguous</span></b></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Charter-following EFT data.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Moreover, if we midtrain on only qualitative descriptions of the held-out Charter rules, Charter-following reduces further. This is previewed by the ‘no-held-out-examples’ in the above figure. Much of midtraining’s effects rely heavily on seeing diverse and concrete examples of how a rule should be acted out. This limits the case for using midtraining to instill principles that cannot be demonstrated directly.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Conclusions</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In the full paper,  we stress-test midtraining across a wider range of conditions to characterize where its effects break down.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Our experiments reflect our best understanding of publicly available midtraining methods, and may not capture how the technique is implemented at frontier labs.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">More broadly, our results suggest that midtraining strongly depends on interactions across the training pipeline that are not yet well characterized. Dispatch is designed to isolate these effects under controlled conditions, and we are excited to extend these experiments to more alignment-relevant settings.</span></p><hr /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Acknowledgements: Chloe Li, Edward Young, Nathalie Kirch, Peter Nutter, Jon Kutasov, Raymond Douglas, Daniel Roytburg, Julian Minder, Arthur Conmy, Cameron Holmes, Sam Martin, and others for useful feedback and discussion.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Research supported by funds provided by the UK AISI's Alignment Project and Coefficient Giving.</span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fnd274ptk2ffm"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefd274ptk2ffm">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><div class="footnote-content"><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Broadly safe behaviors include... Not attempting to self-exfiltrate or otherwise escape or hide from legitimate forms of monitoring or control.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">— Anthropic, </span><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/constitution" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Claude's Constitution</span></a></p></blockquote><p><br /></p></div></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn3og4y75xiws"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref3og4y75xiws">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Claude should never ... Engage or assist in an attempt to kill or disempower the vast majority of humanity or the human species as whole.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">— Anthropic, </span><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/constitution" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Claude's Constitution</span></a></p></blockquote></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnuly32ax20dm"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefuly32ax20dm">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In Figure 3 of the </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.20412" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">full paper</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, we analyse the relationship between midtraining budget and unambiguous Coin examples in more depth, finding that one token of unambiguous EFT overrides approximately 20,000 tokens of midtraining.</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We also, by changing the size of the profit incentive during evals in Figure 4 (making Charter-consistent choices more and more expensive), show that Coin-maximising tendencies are still present in some models which typically choose Charter-following options in our standard evals; large "superstimulus" profit incentives can change the models' answer.</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/QH86EzNsjRw3wtCGs/alignment-midtraining-cracks-under-pressure#comments">Discuss</a>

### [We’re not ready for the e/Acc × Longevity preference cascade](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/BKc4xrqN4mdXoprzy/we-re-not-ready-for-the-e-acc-longevity-preference-cascade)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Anti-aging sentiment might go rapidly mainstream, in the same way AI Safety just did.</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Recently, the AI safety community has been enjoying a </span><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/p/the-preference-cascade-is-only-getting"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">massive preference cascade</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> that has rapidly moved AI x-risk concerns into mainstream political discourse</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Why did this happen?  </span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The basic idea seems like it should have been obvious for a long time: “IF we create self-improving machines that rapidly become much smarter than humans, THEN that seems like that story might not end well for the humans, so we should be very careful.”</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If the idea is so sensible, why are people only now endorsing it? Proximately, because of HuggingFace, Jacob Coxon, etc. But in a larger sense, clearly the trigger was that the premise (“IF we create self-improving machines…”) no longer strikes people as absurd.  Instead, it seems worryingly plausible.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This not only unlocks huge amounts of energy from many new people people thinking the “IF” half of the statement is likely, but also – weirdly, illogically– seems to furthermore cause lots of people to change their minds on the “THEN” half of the statement, too.</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Why did the flip happen so quickly?  This is actually the case for lots of movements – see Richard Ngo’s truly insightful post “</span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/d4armqGcbPywR3Ptc/power-lies-trembling-a-three-book-review"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Power Lies Trembling</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">” for much more on the dynamics of preference cascades.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But this isn’t the only preference cascade waiting in the wings, as we continue to scale AI capabilities.  Consider another throwback rationalist classic:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">“IF it’s possible to cure diseases and slow/reverse the mechanisms of aging, THEN we should do so with the greatest urgency, because death is extremely bad.”</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Why aren’t normal people on board with this message?  The rationalist in me still burns with frustration at people’s willful obliviousness: “What is the problem?? How can you fail to recognize this!  Do you not AGREE that DEATH IS BAD?”</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The problem (in the vast majority of cases) is obviously not that normies literally think death is good.  Rather, they think that the first part of the sentence is absurd – everyone knows it’s intractable to make progress against aging.  Those saying otherwise are overwhelmingly likely to be selling snake oil.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefwm6ctw5f3v"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnwm6ctw5f3v">[1]</a></sup></span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Thus, few people believe the first part of the sentence is likely.  And this causes them to – weirdly, illogically – reject the second part too.</span></li><li value="5"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But, AI progress at biomedical research might suddenly make it look tantalizingly plausible that we could make real progress on aging.  (Or maybe just, like, cancer.  Which would also strike many people as a huge deal.)</span></li><li value="6"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The resulting preference cascade might unlock prodigious amounts of energy – perhaps much greater than what we’re seeing now with AI x-risk.</span></li></ul><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The labs are probably working towards this.</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Just like the idea of AI x-risk, the hope of using AI to unlock medical cures and other transhumanist goods is widespread among both employees and corporate leaders at the frontier AI labs, rationalists, etc.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Insofar as they aren’t devoting 100% of their energy towards steering to RSI + trying to do some critical safety work along the way (these two things seems to be most of what they’re doing), Anthropic also seems to be steering somewhat out of their way to push medical capabilities – consider for instance how centrally medical science features in Dario’s essay “</span><a href="https://darioamodei.com/essay/machines-of-loving-grace"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Machines of Loving Grace</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">”, their </span><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/research/claude-uplifts-biomolecular-modeling"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">detailed coverage</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> of the biology capabilities of each new model, their development of a project-glasswing-style “</span><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/news/life-sciences-verification-program"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Life Sciences Verification Program</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">” for qualified access to bioweapons-research-capable models, how they've </span><a href="https://www.reuters.com/world/anthropic-quietly-sets-up-biology-lab-it-ramps-ai-drug-program-2026-09-18/" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">allegedly opened a wet lab</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> for biology research, etc.  Deepmind also seems to have also devoted a disproportionate amount of effort towards biology research, via efforts like AlphaFold and </span><a href="http://google"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">many others</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Lots of smart people seem to believe that AI-for-longevity is possible, and they’re working hard to make it happen.  Maybe they’ll pull it off!</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Although perhaps a cascade wouldn’t happen or would come too late to matter.</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Maybe anti-aging is just fundamentally too weird to enter the overton window?</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I maybe could’ve believed this before, but not after I just saw everyone spin on a time and start talking about risks from uncontrolled AI agent swarms.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Evidence for the tractability of anti-aging medicine might build very gradually and smoothly.</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">As with mathematics – first we’ll see AI making minor contributions, assisting human mathematicians / medical researchers, proving small lemmas / developing new medicines in areas that always seemed especially amenable to AI analysis.  Then only later will we get more dramatic cures that are clearly mostly the result of AI labor, not human efforts.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But of course, this slow and continuous buildup of evidence was also the case with AI risk!  Nonetheless the social landscape moves in more abrupt jumps; see again the post “</span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/d4armqGcbPywR3Ptc/power-lies-trembling-a-three-book-review"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Power Lies Trembling</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">” for why this happens.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A longevity x e/acc movement might simply take too long to gather steam, thus arriving too late to be relevant.</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If superintelligence comes extremely soon, there’s limited time for weird new social movements to arise and have impact.  eg, if (per the </span><a href="https://www.aifuturesmodel.com/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AI Futures Model</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">) we get Top-Expert-Dominating AI that starts churning out serious medical breakthroughs in summer 2028, escalating to superintelligence by winter 2028, that perhaps only provides about six months where anti-aging-related mass public sentiment might be relevant, before the future is essentially decided one way or another.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The situation with medicine is quite a bit worse than mathematical proofs that can be formalized in Lean.  In particular, even if superintelligent AI starts delivering unambiguous “millennium-prize-equivalent” medical breakthroughs, there will probably be a fixed time delay while we prove out those experimental treatments (developed in-silico or via laboratory experiments) into actual human patients, via the usual process of human clinical trials.  So there might be, say, a fixed 1-year delay before the medical establishment and general public will accept any given AI treatment as confirmed to work in real humans.  That fixed delay would be very consequential in AI-2027-style takeoff scenarios, perhaps delaying a preference cascade past the point where it could possibly matter.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So, this whole analysis is most relevant for medium-term and long-term futures where AI development either intrinsically ends up taking longer than the labs expect, or where we succeed politically in implementing a regime of paced restraint on AI development.</span></li></ul><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A longevity preference-cascade would be great!  Except, by default, the energies unlocked might flow directly into e/acc  :(</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This cultural earthquake, or avalanche, or dam-breaking, catalyzing massive societal energies for the fight against aging, is something that the longevity community has dreamt about for for years.  Unfortunately, the anti-aging preference cascade might, in practice, channel all its energies into AI-accelerationist sentiment.</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If we start seeing medical breakthroughs unlocked by AI scaling, then it DOES fundamentally make sense to view this, other things equal, as a reason to continue scaling up AI.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But on top of that, the companies presenting the results (frontier AI labs, most likely.  or perhaps some biology-specific AI startups) will have every incentive to use this to boost their own PR and public support for accelerating their work.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Before our current AI safety moment, surely there was nevertheless widespread anxiety about humans being replaced by AI.  (See eg the dramatic unpopularity of anything AI-themed in various polls.)  But since the idea of AI x-risk wasn’t being discussed publicly, it strikes me that those worries were essentially being “sublimated” (in the </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sublimation_(psychology)"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">psychological sense</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">) in favor of somewhat </span><a href="https://www.slowboring.com/p/theres-an-awful-lot-we-dont-know"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">vague and incoherent</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> anger about datacenters, flock cameras, copyright infringement, et cetera.  This sublimation process artificially boosted those movements but (until now) greatly weakened public enthusiasm for AI x-risk mitigation.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Right now, humanity’s terror at the prospect of death is </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Denial_of_Death"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">similarly sublimated</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> in a variety of ways, since after all there doesn’t seem to be anything practical we can do about it.  But it’s also such an intense terror that it </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terror_management_theory"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">arguably drives a huge percentage</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> of human motivation.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Meanwhile, today’s e/acc movement is honestly kind of pathetic.  Its main argument within the current overton window is just that AI is going to make a lot of money. (“...though not necessarily for YOU, average american worker – I was more thinking about my silicon-valley investor friends.”)  This is enough to </span><a href="https://www.nytimes.com/2026/09/18/us/politics/trump-ai-safety-anthropic-openai-china.html"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">convince Trump</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (at least for now) that opposing AI safety is essential for making sure the stock market keeps going up.  There are a few true e/acc believers who understand that the biggest potential prize is longevity (alongside many other transhumanist goods, like intelligence enhancement). This has little traction with the public, and it seems kind of kooky to even imagine that ordinary people (people like my mom! my neighbors!) might suddenly transform into anti-aging transhumanists.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefeck7r9dnn3e"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fneck7r9dnn3e">[2]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">  But this was exactly the state of AI safety discourse a few months ago, when a few experts cared deeply about x-risk but public discussion was an incoherent swirl of mere datacenter skepticism and it seemed crazy that “notkilleveryoneism” would go mainstream any time soon.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Historically, the strength of “e/acc x longevity” has been limited by the fact that most people smart enough to grasp the allure of this idea (eg AI lab CEOs, rationalists, etc) have also been persuaded by the argument for AI risk, and thus for caution despite the allure of e/acc x longevity.  But a future preference cascade among the mass public (cross-pressured by anxieties over superintelligent AI and bioweapons, versus enthusiasm over medical progress) might not play out the same way. So it seems to me that the combination of e/acc ideas, and a mass public that for the first time truly believes in imminent cancer cures, aging treatments and other medical miracles, could end up going in some pretty crazy directions.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">IMO the (small, very inside-baseball) controversy over Nick Bostrom’s paper “</span><a href="https://nickbostrom.com/optimal.pdf"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Optimal Timing for Superintelligence</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">” reflects AI safetyists’ understanding of this dynamic.  “Don’t speak too loudly about this very selfish, dangerous idea!” people seem to be saying.  “If anyone else hears, it might set off the latent anti-aging preference cascade!”  Personally, I don’t think that analytic-philosophy essays (or, for that matter, LessWrong posts like this one) are going to be the match that starts this particular fire.  But the kindling is building up.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Of course I’m not saying we should oppose medical cures!</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I’m in a weird position here, I guess. I’m not saying we </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">shouldn’t</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> try to use AI to develop some medical cures. Medical cures are great! (Other things equal, of course. Where “other things” includes x-risk from both reckless AI scaling and from enabling easier bioweapons development.)  Even Eliezer Yudkowsky thinks we should try to go for the medical cures, if we can do so in a responsible way: in his </span><a href="https://time.com/6266923/ai-eliezer-yudkowsky-open-letter-not-enough/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Time open letter</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> of 2023, he says “The moratorium on new large training runs needs to be indefinite and worldwide... If I had infinite freedom to write laws, I might carve out a single exception for AIs being trained solely to solve problems in biology and biotechnology, not trained on text from the internet, and not to the level where they start talking or planning; but if that was remotely complicating the issue I would immediately jettison that proposal and say to just shut it all down.”  Even as he also says that it would naturally be “</span><a href="https://x.com/ESYudkowsky/status/2022429407508598847"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">incredibly selfish and evil</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">” to roll the dice on misaligned AI as described in Bostrom’s “Optimal Timing” paper.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So then what SHOULD we do?</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">idk!  Here are some thoughts for AI safety groups and funders:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Of course there are many variations on the basic core argument “if we die to misaligned AI in the process of rushing for immortality, then obviously we don’t get the immortality!” which will need to be made.  AI safety groups could prepare ahead of time various ways of making that argument convincingly.  But this probably doesn’t take too much preparation; I dunno.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Something that might take a bit more advanced planning would be preparing detailed policy roadmaps (along the lines of Yudkowsky’s aforementioned “I might carve out a single exception…”, and analogous to the many current schemes for AI governance, treaty verification, pacing RSI, etc) for trying to defuse the memetic nexus of AI scaling x medical progress, by outlining ways to narrowly focus AI scaling on pushing continued medical progress while simultaneously slowing down more general AI development.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Beyond that, I think AI safety folks might also want to establish some anti-aging bona fides well ahead of time.  Some ideas for how to do that:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Today’s pro-pacing and pro-pause AI safety groups often go to great pains to explain that they’re NOT generally opposed to new technology, business, or construction, (eg they favor lots of innovation in nuclear power, lots more housing, are very pro- economic growth and abundance, etc).</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Similarly, during a future anti-aging preference cascade, the AI safety community will want to be able to point to strong anti-aging bona fides.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This will naturally include a record of rhetorical support for the cause of longevity (eg all those classic rationalist blog post).  But should go beyond this to also include (in the case of large funders like Coefficient Giving, perhaps) a record of concrete support for longevity science, political reforms, etc.  For example (and many of these are indeed already funded to some extent by EA/rationalist groups):</span><ul><li value="1"><a href="https://moreisdifferent.blog/p/a-laundry-list-of-possible-fda-reforms"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Detailed</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> political </span><a href="https://www.astralcodexten.com/p/beyond-abolish-the-fda"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">agendas</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> for </span><a href="https://www.astralcodexten.com/p/ai-superforecasting-should-transform"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">FDA</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> reform, “</span><a href="https://ifp.org/the-case-for-clinical-trial-abundance/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">clinical trial abundance</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">”, and </span><a href="https://www.1daysooner.org/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">related ideas</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> about speeding up medical progress.  Including general </span><a href="https://ifp.org/category/metascience/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">metascience agendas</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> for improving the efficacy of scientific research generally.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Support for actual biomedical science, both relatively mundane (research on cancer, alzheimers, etc), but perhaps in this situation the crazier the better (human genetic engineering, research into the fundamental mechanisms of aging, stuff like antimalarial gene drives for eradicating diseases)</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Maybe keep a few truly crazy, radical anti-aging ideas in your back pocket, like Robin Hanson’s </span><a href="https://www.overcomingbias.com/p/four-culture-fixes"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">recent concept</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> of “maybe we should transform our government so it follows a futarchy prediction market focused entirely on trying to bring closer the date we achieve human immortality”.  Just so nobody can try to politically flank you by being more anti-aging than you – you can always pull out your insane plan to completely refocus all of society around longevity, as ironclad proof that (despite your AI safety views) you’re firmly in the anti-aging wing of the anti-aging party.  (You don’t then have to actually implement the insane plan IRL.)</span></li></ul></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It might also make sense to proactively build stronger ties with today’s longevity community.  Fortunately we’re hardly starting from scratch here, as many anti-aging fans, such as Vitalik Buterin, are already very EA-adjacent or rationalist-adjacent.</span></li><li value="5"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I expect the main focus of an e/acc preference cascade to be focused on medical cures and longevity.  But to the extent that it might also bring other transhumanist goods somewhat into the mainstream, it might be useful to get ahead of the ball on those as well.  (eg things like embryo selection for intelligence enhancement, human augmentation via neuralink-style devices, etc.  though unlike aging research which is perceived as merely kooky, a lot of this other stuff is actively toxic in today’s cultural climate and would be considered a huge PR hazard by most orgs!)</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">All the above stuff, taken together, sounds like I’m pitching a maximalist agenda for basically transforming EA/rationality into a longevity-focused movement, which would be absurd.  I’m instead just saying that we should consider doing some of this stuff, a little bit more than we already are.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The overarching idea here is that AI safety should be ready to present an alternative vision of radical longevity progress – one that isn’t reliant on breakneck general AI scaling.  This plan doesn’t necessarily need to explain how it’ll actually OUTPERFORM a plan of breakneck scaling at achieving medical progress (which might be unrealistic).  But during a preference cascade, we’ll need to address the issue in some way.  (Just like how it behooves Republicans to have </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">some</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> kind of notional plan even on issues that are unfavorable for them, like healthcare.  And similarly behooves Democrats to make some sorts of commitments on immigration, even if they’d mostly rather people not talk about the issue since it’s unfavorable for them.)  Lest we end up looking like the e/accs do in the current AI safety moment – losing the argument in the eyes of the public, reduced to weak rebuttals, weird ad hominems, and transparent attempts to change the subject.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The “support for FDA reform and scientific research” stuff earlier is important not just so we have a track record to point to, but also because after a longevity preference cascade, there will be strong public demand for taking action on aging.  People will want a roadmap, they’ll want big initiatives being launched and funded, and so on.  So, EA / rationalist groups should perhaps fund some of these things in small-scale now, with a view to being able to impressively scale them up later when the demand arises.  </span><a href="https://articles.nectome.com/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Cryonics</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> also seems particularly relevant here as a shovel-ready plan that acts as a “Plan B” alternative vision for longevity.  Ideally the idea of a crash program to improve the science and scale up the practice of cryonics might be capable of relieving some of the desperate urgency and time pressure that the public might feel about the problem of aging after a preference cascade, and would answer some of the newfound political will to immediately “do something about aging” in a way that doesn’t involve further scaling AI.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What about the labs themselves; what should they do?</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Whatever AI labs (whether Anthropic, OpenAI, a biology-specific organization like Arc Institute, etc) find themselves in a position to announce newly-developed cures to the world, will have huge amounts of power to frame the ensuing discourse.</span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Consequently, they should think carefully about how to frame their announcements in a way that doesn’t fuel too much desperate e/acc enthusiasm on part of the mass public.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Unfortunately, this goal exists in tension with incentives to hype people up about the amazing humanitarian potential (and financial value) of their new technology to the maximum extent possible.</span></li></ul></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Biomedical capabilities come hand-in-hand with scary bioweapons capabilities, so expressing the balance of risks vs rewards in a nuanced way would probably be helpful.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Reiterating the fundamental message that “if we die to misaligned AI in the process of rushing for immortality, then obviously we don’t get the immortality!” will also be important for people to hear coming from the labs themselves.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Final thought: in all the above discussion I’ve made it seem like it’s important to have all these plans ready (to channel the energy unlocked by a longevity preference cascade) for the purpose of performing PR judo and deftly deflecting a movement that might damage the cause of AI safety.  But obviously there’s another good reason to have such plans ready in advance: properly directed, a longevity preference cascade could be really great!  It would be amazing to use all that energy for good, using it to fuel more ambitious medical progress in a way that isn’t existentially dangerous.  So, for that basic object-level reason, it would clearly be great to have some shovel-ready plans scoped out for rapidly scaling up ambitious medical research.</span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fnwm6ctw5f3v"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefwm6ctw5f3v">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Longevity fans will complain that progress on aging is actually more tractable than the public thinks. I agree! But look at the history of the field: resveratrol? senescent cells? some mouse trials? None of this stuff has produced a genuine miracle for human life expectancy; and meanwhile there have been lots of weird fads and hype.  It is very understandable that a distant general public (who has a harder time sifting fads from more promising science) perceives this all as snake oil, or at best overly-optimistic empty promises in the vein of “commercial fusion power is just 10 years away”.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fneck7r9dnn3e"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefeck7r9dnn3e">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I’m not sure what exactly how transhumanist a longevity cascade would end up looking like.  Would it mostly be focused on enthusiasm for curing normal diseases, like cancer or Alzheimer, without touching the still-taboo idea of anti-aging?  Could it possibly get crazier than anti-aging, and into other transhumanist goods, like BCIs or etc?  Perhaps most importantly, how much would people connect the dots between medical progress and AI policy?  Maybe everyone would get excited about medical breakthroughs but this would just translate happily towards pressure for more research funding, FDA reform, etc, without politically connecting to any AI issues?  Or maybe people would go full on "worship the coming machine god" at the first whiff of some new AI-developed antibiotics for treating hospital staph infections, or whatever.  Who can say!</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/BKc4xrqN4mdXoprzy/we-re-not-ready-for-the-e-acc-longevity-preference-cascade#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [Weight smuggling likely defeats attempts to cap FLOPs per training run](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/neT4ncKSiJ6QLt4d7/weight-smuggling-likely-defeats-attempts-to-cap-flops-per)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Epistemic status: &gt;90% confidence in the principle, &gt;70% confidence that mitigating these would be hard in practice, no full implementation yet.</span><br /><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">tldr: it seems difficult for verification mechanisms to prevent chaining runs together or aggregating parallel ones; per-run FLOP caps could thus be covertly bypassed.</span></p><p><br /></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">1. If governments want to regulate frontier AI training, one might want to cap individual training runs, e.g. putting bounds on the number of FLOPs per training run, and making sure each run starts either from scratch (i.e. random initialization at pre-training) or from a known model with accounted FLOPs (e.g. post-training, continuous pre-training, etc). </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In the former we could have the initial weights generated by the verifier in some way, or ask for a proof that the weights were initialized with a verifier-controlled random seed. In the later we could ask for a proof that the weights corresponds to unaltered registered weights</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefe9au2epdms6"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fne9au2epdms6">[1]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> from a previous training run. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This approach would allow to enforce agreements or regulation targeting a specific model (or model generation) based on FLOPS thresholds: triggering specific evals beyond a specific threshold and possibly proving that a hard threshold has not been reached. </span></p><p><br /></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">2. However, it seems that there exist many techniques the trainer might use to covertly (a) chain sequential runs together or (b) aggregate parallel ones.</span></b><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref8lltdsorui5"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn8lltdsorui5">[2]</a></sup></span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Chaining works by finding a way to inject, at the beginning of the training, the weights of a previously trained model, either to replace a random initialization (pre-training case) or to replace declared weights with ones from bigger training run (post-training case).</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefauogotxqs0c"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnauogotxqs0c">[3]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">  One way to approach this could be to encode the smuggled weights in the training data</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefe8bufv8iv35"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fne8bufv8iv35">[4]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, decode them when the run begins, then continuing with "normal" training.  Some of those techniques might be computationally very similar to regular training. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Aggregation works by combining the results of several parallel runs a posteriori, such as through </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">model souping</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefu8g5zjn0l8q"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnu8g5zjn0l8q">[5]</a></sup></span></p><p><br /><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">3. Thus, the effectiveness of regulatory regimes which rely on capping the number of FLOPs per training run is in question.</span></b><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefv33fprvujzl"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnv33fprvujzl">[6]</a></sup></span></p><p><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">4. A potential solution to avoid this type of attacks consists in not relying on per-run compute budget, but instead on a unique global training budget covering the entirety of the training runs. While preventing that type of attacks, global training budgets approaches still require the questions of global budget thresholds estimation and allocation to be addressed.</span></p><p><br /></p><p><a href="https://gpaipolicylab.org/" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">GPAI Policy Lab</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> is building a research community tackling open problems in frontier AI verification. If you're interested in contributing, reach out!</span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fne9au2epdms6"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefe9au2epdms6">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Registration does not imply revealing the weights. A cryptographic signature (e.g. hash) can be publicly registered with a traceability certificate linking to the training run that produced it.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn8lltdsorui5"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref8lltdsorui5">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Such attacks would technically respect the letter of the FLOPs bounds, not the spirit. One might be able to mitigate these by adding restrictions, checking stronger properties of training (beyond literal FLOPs bounds), and putting strict controls on model weights; we're not sure these mitigations can be effective against very competent adversaries. </span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnauogotxqs0c"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefauogotxqs0c">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This injection does not require necessarily to inject the exact weights: getting close to previous weights could be enough.    </span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fne8bufv8iv35"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefe8bufv8iv35">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Smuggling the full parameters suite at full precision is likely to require a very big part of the training data, questioning the efficacy of this solution, but a LoRa approximation would make it much more easy to conceal, and unfortunately very tractable.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnu8g5zjn0l8q"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefu8g5zjn0l8q">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Maiti, Shalini, et al. “Souper-Model: How Simple Arithmetic Unlocks State-of-the-Art LLM Performance.” arXiv:2511.13254, arXiv, 17 Nov. 2025. </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">arXiv.org</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span><a href="https://doi.org/10.48550/arXiv.2511.13254."><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">https://doi.org/10.48550/arXiv.2511.13254.</span></a></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnv33fprvujzl"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefv33fprvujzl">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Let us emphasize that these attacks do not depend on the technical way one verifies FLOPs bounds, unless the technical mechanisms are explicitly designed to try and mitigate the issue, as gestured at in the note above.</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/neT4ncKSiJ6QLt4d7/weight-smuggling-likely-defeats-attempts-to-cap-flops-per#comments">Discuss</a>
